I am informed that today's meeting cannot be attended by the following Member and Members: Dr Simona Kustec Lipicer, mag. Tanja Zink, Mrs Janja Sluga, Mrs Iva Dimic from 16:00 on, Mrs Irena Grošelj Kosnik until 2.30 p.m., Dr. Matej T. Vatovec, Dr. Franc Trček, Mr Roberto Battelli and Mr Marjan Dolinšek. I welcome everyone here. We're moving to nine. At the end of the day, this is first of all a proposal for a law on amendments to the Bank of Slovenia Act. The draft law was tabled by a group of 21 Members and Members, with first-signed Mr Jozet Tank. With regard to this bill, a group of 20 Members and Members, with first-signed Jozet Tank, requested that the National Assembly hold a general debate. ParlaMint-SI_2015-03-31-SDZ7-Redna-06.ana.u2	Mr President, thank you for saying so. Dear Secretary of State, colleagues and colleagues! Slovenia is the only one of the transition countries that has restored the banking system for the second time at the expense of taxpayers. The last recovery cost taxpayers more than €5 billion and is a question of whether this is the final sum or not. In the Slovenian Democratic Party, we are convinced that part of the responsibility for the situation in the Slovenian banking system is also borne by the regulator, Banka Slovenije. Despite the fact that the European Central Bank has now become a supervisor over the three largest system banks, we are confident that Banka Slovenije will still play a very important role in the banking system and will also depend on Banka Slovenije as a result of the operation of banks in Slovenia, and directly or indirectly will also affect the state of the Slovenian economy. In the Slovenian Democratic Party, we are convinced that the Bank of Slovenia Act needs additions and changes. The objectives and principles of the proposed law are, in particular, to increase or ensure transparency of operation and to establish the democratic responsibility of the Bank of Slovenia or its organs, namely, the Governor and the Council of the Bank of Slovenia. The bill follows the principle, transparency, transparency and accountability of public institutions and their bodies. I will continue to present the main solutions brought by the proposed law and, of course, more explanations for individual changes in the discussion below. Banka Slovenije has the exact same report for both the National Assembly and the general public, which means that Banka Slovenije shares responsibility and reporting to the National Assembly with the general public, although the Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia and the laws do not give rise to this. We therefore propose in the Slovenian Democratic Party that an article be supplemented by a provision that the report for the National Assembly should be comprehensive and, if necessary, should include confidential information and information. The Act on Banka Slovenije provides, inter alia, that the Council of the Bank of Slovenia shall regulate the rights and obligations arising from working relations for the members of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia. Therefore, the members of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia determine their own remuneration and other rights from the working relationship, which is disputed for the Slovenian Democratic Party. The public has been repeatedly appalled by the extraordinary receipts of members of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia and is expected to be better addressed by the cooperation of the European Central Bank. However, the current Banka Slovenije Act also stipulates that the members of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia find themselves also incompatible in the performance of the functions of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia, not only controversial but also unethical. The representatives of the Committee of the National Assembly responsible for Finance and Monetary Policy and the Minister responsible for Finance may participate in the meetings of the Bank of Slovenia, but do not have the right to vote. We believe in the Slovenian Democratic Party that there is a need to ensure greater transparency and democraticism in the functioning of the Bank of Slovenia, and therefore, with the proposed amendment, we propose that the Council of the Bank of Slovenia may also be represented by the President of the Commission for the supervision of public finances, which means that the opposition would have its representative, of course without a vote. The draft law proposes to amend the provisions on the number of terms of office of the Governor and Vice-Governors and on the appointment and extension of the members of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia. Thus, the governors and vice-governors could be reappointed at the most once, the extension of the members of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia could be proposed by the President of the Republic and decided by the National Assembly with a two-thirds majority of all Members. A two-thirds majority of all Members would also be appointed members of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia. In order to ensure a more efficient operation of the Bank of Slovenia, it is proposed that the Governor may at any time, without reason, dismiss the Director or Deputy Director of the Bank of Slovenia. In the Slovenian Democratic Party, we are convinced that all these changes would improve the functioning of the Bank of Slovenia, allow for more effective supervision and, above all, that the result will be [[…]] [[sign for the end of the debate]] Better operation of banks in Slovenia. [[…]]
ParlaMint-SI_2015-03-31-SDZ7-Redna-06.ana.u3	For an introductory explanation of the opinion, I give the floor to the representative of the Government, State Secretary at the Ministry of Finance, Mrs Irena Sodin.
ParlaMint-SI_2015-03-31-SDZ7-Redna-06.ana.u4	Dear Presidents, honourable Members and Members of Parliament! The National Assembly is today discussing a proposal for a law on amendments and additions to the Bank of Slovenia Act, which has been tabled by a group of Members and Members, with the first-signed Mr Jozet Tank. Allow me to briefly present the opinion of the Government of the Republic of Slovenia on this proposal. The Government of the Republic of Slovenia notes that the proposed amendments and additions to the Bank of Slovenia Act affect the independence of the Bank of Slovenia as set out in the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union and the Statute of the European System of Central Banks and of the European Central Bank. However, the Government of the Republic of Slovenia considers the following with regard to specific amendments to the law. With the addition of Article 26 of the Bank of Slovenia Act regulating the reporting of Bank of Slovenia to the National Assembly, the report of Bank of Slovenia must be comprehensive and, in so far as it is necessary to include confidential information and information. The Government of the Republic of Slovenia believes that Banka Slovenije's reporting is already properly and comprehensively regulated and therefore no changes are necessary in this area. Regarding the information provided by representatives of the National Assembly on the work of the Bank of Slovenia, the Government of the Republic of Slovenia estimates that representatives of the National Assembly have good opportunities to obtain information from the Bank of Slovenia. In accordance with the Banka Slovenije Act in force, a representative of the Committee on Finance and Monetary Policy of the National Assembly may participate in meetings of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia. This competence of the National Assembly in terms of obtaining relevant information is actually stronger than the regular reporting to the National Assembly as proposed by the law-makers. The Government of the Republic of Slovenia also opposes the proposal that the governors and vice-governors should be re-appointed at the most once, since it considers that there are no legitimate reasons for such a limitation of the number of governors and vice-governors' mandates. The appointment of governors and vice-governors is under the jurisdiction of the National Assembly. In doing so, the Member shall be given the opportunity not to appoint the Governor or Vice-Governor who failed to perform his work again. The Government of the Republic of Slovenia also disagrees with the proposal to require a two-thirds majority of all members of the National Assembly to appoint a member of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia. The current arrangements under which a majority of all Members are required for appointment shall be appropriate and comparable to those laid down in other comparable laws, such as the Constitutional Court Act and the Court of Auditors Act. The law in force concerning the discharge of members of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia provides that a member of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia may be released prematurely only if he so requests discharge, if there are grounds for incompatibilities during the term of office, or if it is established within the prescribed period that he no longer fulfils the conditions required for the performance of his duties or has committed a serious breach. In accordance with the law, the Regulation governing that procedure was issued by the Bank of Slovenia in agreement with the Minister of Finance. The Government of the Republic of Slovenia notes that the current procedure for early discharge of the Governor is in line with the provisions of the Statute of the European System of Central Banks and of the European Central Bank, which ensure the independence of the decision-making bodies of the national central banks. Therefore, it does not agree with the proposal that the National Assembly should assess whether a member of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia has committed a serious breach of the law or constitution, as this would violate the principle of the independence of the Bank of Slovenia. The Government does not oppose the proposal to provide the Court of Auditors with a legal basis for the revision of the Bank of Slovenia from the point of view of the use of public funds, but an appropriate basis must be provided in the Court of Auditors Act, taking into account the requirements set out in its opinion by the European Central Bank. On the basis of the above and on the basis of the opinion of the European Central Bank, the Government does not support the proposed law and considers it not appropriate for further consideration.

The European Central Bank and the Bank of Slovenia are school cases where neoliberalism has nothing to do with freedom, but it must always be introduced through administrative, economic and political coercion. So in Europe. If we go to Latin America sometimes even with guns. In Europe, both in the last ten years, we have a power centre that has as much power as any central bank in history. This power centre, as French economist Thomas Piketty recently pointed out, is "always more independent", which in practice means that it is increasingly alienated from citizens and the economy and is therefore so much closer to financial capital interests. Nobody even hides this because the ECB's status is completely clear. The European Central Bank’s task is to ensure price stability, i.e. the security of financial investments to the detriment of development and employment. If we look across the sea into the US, which is the leading capitalist state, their central bank operates on completely different principles. It puts employment first, i.e. lending to the economy and development. Secondly, price stability, but attention, is balanced by limiting interest rates. The high interest rates are a source of income as they shift real-economy investments into financial speculation. As Keynes said, "The renter's euthanasia is a prerequisite for full employment policy." In Banka Slovenije, Keynesa was stopped from reading long ago and certainly did not read his pupil Hyman Minsky, as they would have adhered to these principles and would probably have taken some measure against the overheating of financial markets between 2007 and 2008. If we look at how things have been going after the crisis, we can compare the recovery of the United States after 2010 and the calvary that Europe has experienced over the years, we need to make it clear to everyone whether the Ordoliberals in Frankfurt or the Keynesians in New York are right. The difference between one GDP and another is that the US today has 108% of pre-crisis GDP and 99% of the EU's monetary policy. The EU has not even exceeded its 2007 GDP or if we compare it with the US, it means that the EU has lost 726 billion GDP since 2010 due to its monetary and fiscal policies. Such a system of European central banking is, of course, appropriate for some. It is in line with the most powerful European countries, as it creates Europe at two speeds. This is how Germany today, if I illustrate a little bit, drives by fast train and has exceeded the pre-crisis level of GDP by 3.5%. The periphery, including Slovenia, is forced to drive on the industrial track, so Slovenia's GDP is still 7.3% lower than before the crisis. Of course, we are far from the worst or the slowest. In short, the European System of Central Banks is both on paper and in practice imported and a radical change will be needed. If we look now at Banka Slovenije, which is the matter of today's point. Its symptom is that it must resort to increasingly drastic disciplinary measures and, on the other hand, does not perform its primary functions. Her territory is getting stronger. Our banking system is also centralized, and the result is one big paradox. More than Banka Slovenije has powers, we have less confidence in our banking system. If we look only at some of the powers that Banka Slovenije currently has. Banka Slovenije can now be circumcised for any commercial bank decision. The Bank of Slovenia may withdraw a licence from any member of the Supervisory Board of any bank. It has the power to send the bank, which, according to international accounting standards, has 824 million positive capital, into compulsory settlement, i.e. bankruptcy. We are talking, of course, about the NLB case of 30. 9. 2013 The Bank of Slovenia issues decisions to dissipate owners and creditors. Only in the case of NKBM were there as many as 100,000. The Bank of Slovenia determines the amounts of recapitalisation, co-decisions on the transfer value, the penalties it imposes are increasing. Under the macro-prudential development law and the ever-increasing new banking laws, it will provide for additional capital adequacy, i.e. new barriers to lending, additional costs for banks and committees. If we look at neighbouring Austria, we'll see interesting information. Austria is facing the crisis of its third largest bank today, because Raiffeisen was also distracted by Hyp and Volksbank, and they are now discussing that the capital adequacy of banks would increase from 10% to 12%, and we have the capital adequacy of the two largest banks over 20%, according to NLB and NKBM, over 20% of the capital adequacy and the Bank of Slovenia is still not satisfied. I could count it, but you don't have to. I think it is already clear from this why this institution needs democratic control and democratic guidance. In addition, the objectives and ways of managing monetary policy in Slovenia should also be questioned. Slovenia had, if we look at it in the past, a 15-year-old independent central bank that wisely held a low tolar, compared with the brand and later with the euro. By doing so, it encouraged exports and prevented excessive pressure on wages and employment. This contributed to the fact that between 1992 and 2005 Slovenia was catching up with the old members of the European Union after the social product, without external imbalances. By 2005, Slovenia had actually been a net creditor abroad, public debt had been declining and it had never reached even 30% of GDP, today we have 80. The balance sheet deficit was minimal, there was no inflatable financial and real estate balloons, and unemployment gradually decreased. The Bank of Slovenia, here we see its power, played the role expected of the central bank in the social market economy by different policies at the time of entry into the euro area. It has taken care of growth and employment, not the stability of prices and the security of financial investments, as the central banking system does today. In addition, the banking system is increasingly moving away from the people and following completely wrong policies. The proposals we have on the table today are at least going towards increasing the legitimate control of democratic institutions over the central bank, so we will support this solution in the United Left in the first reading. However, we have comments on certain articles, which we will comment on later in the debate. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2015-03-31-SDZ7-Redna-06.ana.u10	Thank you very much. Your Honor! In May 2014, we discussed the proposal for a law on amendments and additions to the Bank of Slovenia Act. The then-present proponent was not the government as expected. We have been discussing the proposal submitted by the SDS Group today. And it is interesting that almost a year ago they proposed the same or almost the same solutions as today. Let's take a look at some facts. With the adoption of the Bank of Slovenia Act on 25 June 1991, the Bank of Slovenia was established, thus becoming the State Central Bank. Its governing bodies and even the functioning of the Bank of Slovenia are independent, as is the law. It means that, under the Banka Slovenije Act, they are not bound by the decisions, positions and instructions of the state or any authority, nor are they to be addressed in accordance with instructions or guidelines. As from 1 January 2007, the Treaty establishing the European Community and the Statute of the European System of Central Banks and of the European Central Bank of the Bank of Slovenia are also in line with the Treaty establishing the Eurosystem. These contracts and statutes must be fully implemented by the Bank of Slovenia in its operation. The core functions of the Bank of Slovenia are to monitor the financial system and to create and implement the conditions for a stable banking system, the efficient operation of banks and the security of deposits in banks and savings banks. Why such an introduction to the basic functioning of the Bank of Slovenia? Simple. Everything written down clearly states that the amendments to the legislation governing the operation of the Bank of Slovenia cannot be dealt with in a partial and so little way. Well, let's see what the proposers want with the law. First time. More comprehensive reports, even when it comes to banks receiving state aid. Another time. Different arrangements for working relations, namely with regard to salaries and appointments of governors and vice-governors, maximum number of repetitions of mandates and the introduction of possibilities for the dismissal of members of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia. Third time. The enlargement of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia, where, in addition to the Minister of Finance and the President of the Committee on Finance and Monetary Policy, they would also give this authority to the representative of the opposition, namely to the President of the Public Finance Supervisory Commission. Fourth time. Competence of the Court of Auditors with regard to the supervision of the regularity of the operation of the Bank of Slovenia. Fifth. The possibility of disclosing confidential information to banks that have received state aid and would like to obtain this from the bodies of the National Assembly. As last year, the National Assembly also forwarded this proposal to the European Central Bank, which is part of the Bank of Slovenia. The European Central Bank has not assessed positively as it did last year. More. In recent weeks, the European Central Bank has also publicly called on the government to ensure the independence of the Bank of Slovenia. Independence, however, does not provide for the proposed change. More. The proposal for the resolution of the members of the decision-making bodies of the Bank of Slovenia could have a strong impact on independence. The Statute of the European System of Central Banks and of the European Central Bank makes it particularly impossible for the Government or Parliament to decide discreetly on the discharge of the Governor. Would it be possible, given that the European Central Bank has issued a similar opinion last year, to propose an amendment to the Statutes of the European Central Bank and the European System of Central Banks? This would create a basis for a possible third opinion on a possible third proposal of the same or similar law to be positive. As for the more comprehensive report, the European Central Bank also expressed a negative opinion. The requirements, as proposed, would affect the institutional independence of the Bank of Slovenia and the independence of the members of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia. So it's the independence that has to be ensured. The proposed law tabled in this part is also not in line with the previous opinion. ParlaMint-SI_2015-03-31-SDZ7-Redna-06.ana.u13	The text is given by the Slovenian Democratic Party's Parliamentary Group, Mrs Susana Lepi Šimenko.
ParlaMint-SI_2015-03-31-SDZ7-Redna-06.ana.u14	Thank you for your word, chairman. Dear Members, honourable Members, distinguished representatives of the Government! In the Slovenian Democratic Party's Parliamentary Group, we are constantly reminding ourselves of the controversial developments in the banking system and we are working to clear up any irregularities and to properly sanction the irregularities identified. As it has been exposed many times in recent months, the second recovery of the banking system has cost us €5 billion. The reasons for the necessary recovery are in particular the granting of loans to companies and individuals unable to repay them and the granting of loans on friendly terms. Bank administrations led the banks in a non-professional and uneconomic manner and the supervisory authorities did not carry out their tasks. The Bank of Slovenia, which has to carry out the tasks of the regulator effectively, independently, professionally and in accordance with the applicable legislation, has cancelled as the regulator of the banking system. It is the responsibility of the banks to operate in accordance with the legislation, and therefore it is the responsibility of the administrations and supervisory councils of banks for the situation in the Slovenian banking system. When the issue of control and legislation in this area was addressed in 2013 in the European Parliament, Members of the European Central Bank requested that it clarify how the European Central Bank would ensure transparency and accountability in the work of supervisors before the legislation on European banking supervision was approved. Since the new supervisory system carries a large part of its competence from national level to EU level, the European Members requested adequate supervision of the European Central Bank. This led to the conclusion of an agreement between the European Parliament and the European Central Bank to allow the European Parliament to have access to a wide range of information, of course with the appropriate protection of data confidentiality. In recent weeks, we have had a strong media response to the statements made by the owners of decommissioned shares and subordinated bonds of banks that the Bank of Slovenia, in the execution of the rehabilitation of Slovenian banks, has prepared data on capital mismatches in a way that demonstrates negative capital, which was the basis for the European Commission's decision on State aid. Today, it is not clear the methodology or more precisely the input data used in the capital adequacy calculations and were the basis for the deletion. In addition, information on Banka Slovenije's involvement in bank negotiations on the resolution of individual debtors appears in the media in recent days. All of the above justifiably undermines confidence in the regulator of our banking system. Therefore, in the Slovenian Democratic Party's Group, with the proposed amendment, we want to increase the transparency of the operation and establish the democratic responsibility of the Bank of Slovenia or its organs, the Governor and the Council of the Bank of Slovenia. Banka Slovenije forms the same reports for the National Assembly as for the general public, which means that the responsibility and reporting of the National Assembly with the general public is equal. While the Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia provides in Article 152 that Slovenia has a central bank which is independent in its operation and which responds directly to the National Assembly. The Bank of Slovenia Act provides, however, in Article 26(1), that, irrespective of other laws of the Bank of Slovenia, it shall report its work exclusively to the National Assembly and that those reports shall be at least half a year. We propose to supplement this Article with a provision that the report should be comprehensive and that, if necessary, it also includes confidential information and information. In this case, it must comply with the legislation governing the protection of confidential data. The obligation to report promptly to the National Assembly is further laid down if the Bank of Slovenia considers that the Bank or the Custodian needs State aid. The National Assembly is the one that adopts the state budget, which is why it is extremely important. The current law on Banka Slovenije provides, inter alia, that the Council of Banka Slovenije regulates the rights and obligations of working relations for members of Banka Slovenije, so the members of the Council of Banka Slovenije determine their own remuneration and other rights from the working relationship, which we find controversial. The public has also been repeatedly appalled by the high remuneration of members of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia. We therefore expect this issue to be better addressed. It also seems unethical to us that the current Bank of Slovenia law stipulates that the members of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia find themselves incompatible with the performance of their functions. We therefore propose that the external auditor should carry out the examination of the incompatibilities of the performance of the function. A representative of the Committee of the National Assembly responsible for Finance and Monetary Policy and the Minister responsible for Finance may participate in the meetings of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia, but they shall not have the right to vote. This means that a representative of the Government and a representative of the ruling coalition may participate in the meetings of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia, but the representative of the opposition cannot participate. In the Slovenian Democratic Party, we estimate that the participation of the opposition representative at the Council of the Bank of Slovenia meetings would increase the transparency and responsibility of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia, which is why we propose this in the proposed law. The draft law also proposes to amend the provisions on the number of terms of office of the Governor and Vice-Governors and to amend the appointment and discharge of a member of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia. The governor and vice-governors could be re-appointed no more than once. The dismissal of a member of the Council could be proposed by the President of the Republic, and it could be decided by the National Assembly, but not by the ordinary but by the two-thirds majority of all Members. The two-thirds majority would also appoint members of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia. Information on Banka Slovenije's non-economic, inefficient and ineffective business has appeared in the public on several occasions, so we propose that the operation be revised by an external audit company chosen by the National Assembly. The obligation to protect confidential bank data is often used as an excuse to conceal the wrong decisions of bank management, which may sometimes be at the border of legality. Therefore, in the Slovenian Democratic Party we also propose a provision that the obligation to protect confidential data does not apply to data relating to banks and savings banks that have received State aid, or that the bank has been subject to measures under the law on measures taken by the Republic of Slovenia to strengthen the stability of banks. And this data requires a National Assembly, a working body or an investigative commission of the National Assembly. With this in mind, the National Assembly, which has to make important decisions regarding the resolution of problems in the Slovenian banking system, will finally have the opportunity to obtain appropriate data on the state and liability of the situation. With the proposed measures, we want to increase transparency and establish Banka Slovenije's responsibility and, indirectly, help to restore confidence in the regulator of the banking system, and we will, of course, support them in the Slovenian Democratic Party.

ParlaMint-SI_2015-03-31-SDZ7-Redna-06.ana.u16	Dear Mr President, distinguished secretary and co-workers, honourable Members and Members of Parliament, have a good day. Allow me to present to you the position of the Desus Group on the Proposal for a Law on Amendments and Supplements to the Bank of Slovenia Act. We have already held several debates on the outstanding issue of the found irregularities in the operation of the Bank of Slovenia and the rehabilitation of the bank hole in this Parliament, but the topic is still topical precisely because of the fact that the competent are still not responsible for their actions. Without responsibility, there is no progress. We can't just end the story until we've removed the consequences, so we also spend most of our time looking at past actions and forgetting to create the conditions to move forward. As a result, accountability also implies an insight into transparent business, whether it is a private enterprise or a state institution. The rules are the same. The most important provision in this amendment is access to confidential information available to the Bank of Slovenia. Why? Because the professional and even the general public are still wondering what is going on with the taxpayer's money. We call on the Bank of Slovenia to disclose all the data, analyses and procedures that have led to the enormous sum necessary for the rehabilitation of banks. Modesty and incomplete reports, according to the majority, will not suffice. And we look at the average population for a moment, not just those in leading positions. In the Desus Group, we are constantly receiving comments from concerned pensioners who care for every cent of their still-deserved pensions. But here we are talking about billions that are magically disappearing without seeing why and how. It is in these billions that additional amounts for pensions and new jobs are hidden. Sociala suffers from bank recovery, and that is a fact that cannot be disputed. Therefore, it is more than reasonable to ask for information to reveal why this is the case. However, we note that when it comes to confidential information, we must be extremely careful. Surely not everyone can access them just like that. Abuse is a concept that weighs transparency very quickly. Since this is only a general discussion, in the Desus Group, we estimate that in the case of further legislative proceedings, the most complex provision will be the provision allowing the Bank of Slovenia to report more widely to the National Assembly, in particular due to the needs for smooth operation. This would mean that access to data has a competent parent working body, in particular when it deals with matters directly related to decision-making on State ownership, such as consent on privatisation, consent on recapitalisation, audit reports, parliamentary investigations, etc. Quality and timely information is intended to help facilitate and more reasoned decision-making in the adoption of legislative procedures. From the national and European legal order, the Bank of Slovenia must maintain the role of a national banking regulator which ensures effective, independent and professional performance. A mandatory part of the tasks to be carried out is also reporting to the National Assembly on its functioning. The latter is the subject of today's need for an amendment, namely the need to supplement Article 26, which speaks of Banka Slovenije reporting. This increases the possibility for the National Assembly to access the comprehensive report without delay when the Bank of Slovenia requests State aid for the stability of the banking system. In this case, the Bank of Slovenia will have to submit a report to the National Assembly, which will be designed to provide concrete arguments on the reasons, measures and, lastly, the role and responsibility of the bank’s management authorities for the situation. In the Desus Group, we estimate that it would be more appropriate to supplement the provisions on this site to ensure that Banka Slovenije is subject to a mandatory annual review of the regularity of its operations. We believe that it would be much better to regulate these in the Law on the Court of Auditors itself. In its opinion, the European Central Bank was also critical to the general principle of the personal independence of the Bank of Slovenia. The Government also notes in its opinion that the proposals of the proposed amendments to this Act affect the independence of the Bank of Slovenia. It is precisely because of these facts that we will reject in the Desus Group the possibility that the legislative process will continue. However, we expect the Government to bring a law amendment to the legislative process as soon as possible, which will fully and systematically regulate this area. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2015-03-31-SDZ7-Redna-06.ana.u34	Thank you so much for saying that, Mr. President. In the last two months, we have dealt with a lot of possible irregularities in the procedures for the last rehabilitation of banks, from the amendment of banking legislation in 2013 to the implementation procedures for the rehabilitation of banks by Banka Slovenije. However, I will not return to all the evidence already made on the one hand and explain that has been given to us both during these two months by the Government and the Bank of Slovenia, on the other hand, because I believe from here on out that the necessary answers are still the responsibility of these competent institutions and specialised bodies, which must carry out their mission independently of politics and of any other interests. In all of these debates, which we have had on the subject of Banka Slovenije and Banka Sanitation in our Parliament, it seems that even at European Union level, they have been actively engaged in the issue of the independence of Banka Slovenije recently, as warnings come from both the European Commission and the European Central Bank. As early as 2014, the European Central Bank, at the then legislative initiative of members of the National Assembly, which in certain parts is even identical to the amendment that we are considering today, expressed some serious concerns. Some of the amendments proposed today are therefore identical to last year's proposals, which were also tabled by the SDS, on which the European Central Bank has also expressed an opinion last year and the concerns were similar to those of this time. At that time, the legislative initiative in the debate in the National Assembly was not adopted to take account of the concerns raised by the European Central Bank, in particular regarding the protection of the independence of central banks. The European Central Bank has reissued its opinion in response to the proposed amendments reintroduced in the parliamentary procedure by the opposition SDS, in February 2015, due to reports of Banka Slovenije's behaviour in the rehabilitation of banks. The European Central Bank has issued a legal opinion on the proposed amendments to the Bank of Slovenia Act in accordance with its competence under the EU Treaty at the request of the National Assembly. This opinion is public and is also published on the ECB’s websites. What are the proposed changes to which the European Central Bank has responded? Members of the SDS have tabled in the parliamentary procedure a new law on Banka Slovenije, which they want to write down and say, increase the transparency of the functioning of the Slovenian central bank and establish greater responsibility for the Governor and Council of the Bank of Slovenia. To summarize briefly what they are proposing: the larger reporting requirements of Banka Slovenije to the National Assembly for representatives of the opposition to participate in the meetings of the Banka Slovenije Council. Here I can also explain my role in cooperation at the Holy Bank of Slovenia. As President of the Committee on Finance, I get all the materials that are dealt with at the Councils of the Bank of Slovenia and I am informed of what is being adopted and what is being decided at the Councils of the Bank of Slovenia, even if due to any meeting of the Committee or to a meeting of the National Assembly of this Council then I cannot attend. Sveti Banka Slovenije takes place on Tuesdays, but any of the Members can also contact me on the subject of Banka Slovenije, as you are addressing me on other topics, give me initiatives, questions, requests for discussion at a committee meeting on the subject under the responsibility of Banka Slovenije. However, when I assess that, on the basis of a certain topic addressed at the Council of the Bank of Slovenia, a broader discussion is also necessary at the Committee's meeting, I will also convene such a meeting of the Committee and invite representatives of the Bank of Slovenia to attend it. Until now, they have regularly participated in all our meetings of the Committee, not all of them, but where the topic is related to banking, the Bank of Slovenia correctly responds to us, explains and implements the decisions we take on the Committee. Other changes are then made to the resolution procedure of the members of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia, in particular in the event of a serious infringement and incompatibilities of functions, further amendments giving the Governor of the Bank of Slovenia the possibility, without reason, to dismiss the directors and deputy directors of the departments, that the regular functioning of the Bank of Slovenia should be reviewed by the Court of Auditors so that the Governor and the Deputy Governor can be re-appointed at the most once. These changes, which I have briefly summarized here, have been taken by the European Central Bank, as I have said before. And maybe I should touch a few areas myself. First time, revisions. Audit of the Bank of Slovenia is specifically regulated by the Bank of Slovenia Act. In accordance with the provisions of this Act, the Bank of Slovenia shall be audited by an independent international auditor. The Bank of Slovenia's special audit regime was adopted due to its independence, which is determined both by the Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia and by the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union. The revision regime takes into account that the accounts of the European Central Bank and the national central banks are audited by independent external auditors. In our case, the one selected by Banka Slovenije under the Public Procurement Act is proposed for the final selection process, where the Governing Council first deals with it and recommends it to the EU Council, which also approves the auditor. The auditors are selected and operate in accordance with good practices for the selection and mandate of the external auditors designated by the ECB and the requirements for the audit of public interest entities are also governed by the Regulation of the European Parliament and of the Council. However, the Law on the Court of Auditors does not provide for the supervision and supervision of the Bank of Slovenia. Until now, the question of the audit competence of the Court of Auditors was raised once, in 2012, and even then the Court of Auditors did not require a review of the Bank of Slovenia’s operations, but rather a review of the supervisory activities of the Bank of Slovenia. Therefore, claims that no one checks the operation of Banka Slovenije do not hold. The revision is therefore already regulated in the current law in accordance with the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union and the Statutes of the European System of Central Banks and of the European Central Bank. However, if they change the regime currently in force, it should first be necessary to cut down or lay down the legal basis in the Law on the Court of Auditors for the power to revise the Court of Auditors over the regularity of the Bank of Slovenia. In doing so, we should strictly take into account the audit framework presented or reflected in the opinion of the European Central Bank. This provision should first be laid down in the Law on the Court of Auditors. Then forward, a larger reporting role. According to the provisions of the Bank of Slovenia Banka Slovenije Act, Banka Slovenije is already reporting on its work to the National Assembly. I have already said that the representative of the Bank of Slovenia is present at almost every session of the Finance Committee. In this context, the National Assembly is informed of the annual report and financial plan of the Bank of Slovenia. The Bank of Slovenia shall provide all information required by the National Assembly or by the Committee on Finance and Monetary Policy in the absence of explicit legal restrictions. As I said earlier, a representative of the Committee on Finance and Monetary Policy may also participate in a meeting of the Board of the Bank. These, as chairman of the committee, are part of the meetings, and I have called on you earlier for all possible different ways of working than I have for you to give me these initiatives. With regard to reporting to the National Assembly, it should be pointed out that we have also just had a meeting of the National Assembly, where the Governor has fully reported on all the procedures of the previous resolution of the banking system and responded to questions from Members and Members. The current regime therefore ensures the involvement and information of the National Assembly on the work of the Bank of Slovenia. I believe that a different regime could also lead to a certain political subordination of the Bank of Slovenia, or political interference in the operation of the Bank of Slovenia, since no institution may influence the Bank of Slovenia, may give instructions, nor can the Bank of Slovenia refer to other authorities. In the process of assessing the procedures of the Bank of Slovenia, finding infringements of the legislation and, as I said before, other competent authorities are competent. With regard to the transmission of confidential information to the National Assembly, I believe that this area needs to be regulated in accordance with national rules on the protection of confidential data, as well as the rules governing this area at the level of the European Union and in accordance with the ECB's opinion, which the current proposal does not provide. As regards discharge of decision-making bodies. With regard to the resolution of the decision-making bodies of the Bank of Slovenia, the ECB recalls the EU Treaty in Article 130, which sets out the principle of personal independence, which is a key aspect of the principle of the independence of central banks for members of the European System of Central Banks. Under this principle, which is also included in the Statute, the governors can be resolved only if they no longer fulfil the conditions required for the performance of their duties or if they have committed a serious breach. The European Central Bank also points out that any violation of the law or constitution in itself does not constitute a serious breach or is not equivalent to a serious breach and therefore such provisions extend the scope of serious infringements under Union law to such infringements. The relevant national legislator should not define what a worse breach means. The purpose is to prevent the authorities involved in the appointment of governors, in particular the Government or Parliament, from making discretionary decisions regarding the discharge of the Governor, from being alerted to the European Central Bank. They also add that personal independence would be compromised if the same rules were not applicable to other members of the decision-making bodies of the national central bank. Disclosure of classified information. The issue of the extent to which Banka Slovenije can disclose publicly the information it obtains under bank supervision has become acute with exceptional measures. Slovenian and European banking legislation imposes an obligation on the Bank of Slovenia to protect the confidential information it acquires under supervision. In its opinion, the European Central Bank notes that the provisions of the Directive on the exchange of information and business secrets do not provide a legal basis for disclosing classified information to the National Assembly as such. If I finish. On the basis of my view, the proposed amendments to the law lead to a decrease in Slovenia's reputation in this area. In certain segments they violate the Statute of the European System of Central Banks and may interfere with the independence of the Bank of Slovenia. In some segments, the proposal of this law points to the political subordination of the Bank of Slovenia and the decision on powers that can only be determined by a court but not by Members. The European Central Bank also made this clear. I am of the opinion that the regulation of the operation of the central bank cannot be dealt with in a perfect, quick and partial way, as proposed by this bill, and thus jeopardize the institutional dependence of the central bank, so I do not support this bill. Thank you.

But we need to be aware that this needs to be allowed and that the bank is state-owned. The Bank is the property of all of us, and we citizens should all be aware of its business. In the area of the protection of confidential bank data, we propose that this protection should no longer be used as an excuse for bad moves if, of course, they relate to banks that have received state aid. We also propose that the operations of banks should be audited by an external audit firm chosen by the National Assembly, which should then also report on the operation of the bank. So if I sum up, the main objective of the proposed amendments to the Bank of Slovenia Act is to increase or ensure the transparency of the business to which I will say again, in the coalition you like to speak; and to establish the responsibility of the Bank of Slovenia or its authorities. I hope that there will be a change to the law before there will be any further recovery or recapitalisation of banks. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-SI_2015-03-31-SDZ7-Redna-06.ana.u55	Thank you. Mr Luka Mesec has the floor to prepare Marjana Kotnik Poropat.
ParlaMint-SI_2015-03-31-SDZ7-Redna-06.ana.u56	Thank you for your word, Madam Vice President. We have already said in our position in the United Left that in principle the solutions offered by the law or the amendment of the law are supported and that we will therefore also give the law a green light at first reading, because our programme is part of the democratisation of the banking system. We believe that this law that we have before us is taking the first steps towards more effective control of the Bank of Slovenia and further towards some democratic direction of this institution, which has accumulated enormous power in recent years. But I will take the next minute to comment briefly on the concrete solutions that this law offers from our point of view; that is, I will go straight to the articles. If I first stop at Article 26 talking about Banka Slovenije reporting. Here, in our view, the current law is bad, as we have seen mainly with regard to the rehabilitation of banks and the decommissioning of small shareholders and bondholders – we have seen that this system does not work. Banka Slovenije is currently sending reports to the National Assembly, which are essentially a summary of published reports on its website. The key questions we have been asking for months, however, are not answered by the National Assembly and the Slovenian public. Let's say how the liquidity value of banks was assessed, how could the difference of the burden test by the method from below and from above be as much as 45 %, how did they get the amount of the necessary recapitalisation and so on? We still do not get answers to these questions, and it is clear that the existing reporting was not sufficient to enable the National Assembly to carry out its work effectively. We therefore agree with the additions added by the novels, in particular the indent which provides that the report also contains confidential information, if necessary. As we have seen in all the cases described, the Bank of Slovenia has consistently referred to confidentiality. We believe that it is essential for the National Assembly to have access to this information so that it can exercise effective control over this institution. Then let's move on. If I stop at Article 33, this is the participation of other persons at meetings of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia. Novela adds here that the presence of a representative of the Commission for the supervision of public finances would also be ensured at the meetings of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia. Here is the position of the United Left to give the proposed solution too much influence to the two largest parties in the National Assembly. In fact, a representative of the Finance Committee, usually of the coalition, and a representative of the Commission for the supervision of public finances, who is usually of the largest opposition party, would now be sitting in the Council. The two largest parties would thus have an exclusive impact on the flow of information from Banka Slovenije to the National Assembly, so we would rather be invited here to extend Article 26 so that the National Assembly is informed of all that is necessary for the effective exercise of control. The National Assembly here, however, means all Members, not only the two largest groups of Members; of course, in accordance with the rules on data protection. If I go further, to paragraphs 35 and 36, which speak of restricting the terms of office of the Governor and other members of the Bank of Slovenia – we absolutely support this. Otherwise, we think that the problems of our banking system are largely not of a personal nature but of a systemic nature; but we support because in a democratic society it is not normal for someone to be subscribed to a position without any restrictions. We will therefore, of course, support the proposed solution to limit mandates to a maximum of two mandates. If I go to Article 39 talking about the discharges of the members of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia. Here we agree with the view that in the current regime the discharge of a member of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia is virtually impossible because it is very loosely defined. All it says is that this is a serious breach, which is why the Governor can be resolved in order for the Governor to violate the Treaty on European Communities or the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union, which is an extremely broad definition. We therefore agree that it would be more reasonable to have a hearing before the Public Finance Supervisory Commission in case of suspected violations by the Governor or any other member of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia. It is necessary to lay down criteria for what is a violation and what is a worse breach or what these criteria are for the purpose of being resolved by a member. This is not yet in the current order, but we think we can complete this by reading it again. Article 40, then, which refers to the organisation and operation of the Bank of Slovenia. The proposer would add here the possibility that the Governor, without justification, may dismiss the Director or Deputy Director of the Department. We do not agree with this in particular because there is no explanation. The aim of the amendment, as we understand it, is to make the Bank of Slovenia more rational and transparent. If we give the governor these powers to relieve the directors without explanation, then we are going in the opposite direction from the target. We cannot support this article from this point of view; however, we believe that the Governor should always be able to explain his actions when a person is dismissed. And last of all, paragraph 39, no, I apologize – paragraph 25. Here's a talk about a list of entities to be audited by the Court of Auditors. The proposer proposes to include the Bank of Slovenia. We agree with that, but we have two fundamental concerns. With regard to these substantive audits, it is our view that the Court of Auditors already has all the powers to carry out the audit of the Bank of Slovenia. Banka Slovenije is a person of public law and owns 100 % of the country. It fulfils both criteria in the Court of Auditors Act in order for the Court of Auditors to exercise control over this institution. However, another reservation is that it is necessary to distinguish between the audit of annual accounts on the one hand, as investigated by the Court of Auditors, and between the audit or investigation of the profitability and legality of operations on the other hand. And the part that should be resolved by law is in particular the second – revision, investigation of the prudence and legality of business. This is not a task performed by the Court of Auditors. So briefly, these are our comments. As we say, we support the law in its spirit of greater democratic control over the Bank of Slovenia.

We know why this has occurred – because of the granting of loans on friendly lines, because of inadequate insurance and also because of the irresponsible behaviour of bank management, supervisory boards of banks and also the regulator of banks, i.e. Banka Slovenije. In view of the many information that has been available in the media, the issue of transparency in the resolution of the banking system is rightly raised by the general public. In particular, there have been many indications in the area of unusual valuation of bank balances. Also, what was in public a few days ago about Banka Slovenije's involvement in the bank's resolution negotiations is totally unacceptable. In view of the large bank hole, i.e. EUR 5 billion, it can rightly be said that the Bank of Slovenia, as the regulator of the banking system, has failed. The Bank of Slovenia must carry out tasks effectively, independently, professionally and in accordance with the applicable legislation. It is her responsibility to have banks act in accordance with this legislation. We can rightly conclude from this that she is responsible for the situation. We can also rightly ask whether Banka Slovenije's reports are appropriate. Is it not some kind of cover-up that we would not otherwise want to reveal to the general public? Since we still have most state-owned banks in Slovenia, all the costs of this hidden information and consequently poor decisions are going to burden taxpayers. In February, the newspaper The Economist stated that Banka Slovenije is ranked on the highest level according to the number of 100,000 inhabitants; not on the European summit, but on the world summit. According to the number of employees, only the Russian Central Bank is included in front of it and, unlike Banka Slovenije, also performs the tasks of payment transport and monetary policy management. As has already been said, comparison with the Swedish Central Bank, I think it is right to think about these data. The Swedish Central Bank has 4.1 employees per 100,000 inhabitants, while in Banka Slovenije we have an average of 21.6 employees per 100,000 population. Now you will say that Banka Slovenije is an independent body – yes; Article 152 of the Constitution says that it is independent in its operation, but it is directly responsible to the National Assembly. Article 152 of the Constitution states in the 12th paragraph: "Parliament must be able to regularly analyse and control the implementation of monetary policy. The Governor of the Central Bank must always be prepared to explain to Parliament and inform him about all matters of monetary policy." So one is the independence of Banka Slovenije in its operation and the other is the direct responsibility of Banka Slovenije to the National Assembly. There was a lot of doubt as to the confidentiality of the data in the report. The National Assembly is the one adopting the budget, so it is extremely important that it knows what is behind what it is accepting. And if we were talking about some normal situation, if we weren't talking about bailouts with taxpayer money, if we weren't talking about $5 billion, then we'd still understand why there's so much concern about the confidentiality of this data. But anyway, the National Assembly is entitled to this information. And that's exactly why we've proposed the changes we've made [[turn off the microphone]]

ParlaMint-SI_2015-03-31-SDZ7-Redna-06.ana.u82	Thank you for your word. How nice to meet you, Madam Secretary of State! The Law on the organisation and financing of education and education, i.e. ZOFVI, is a statutory law governing education, education from kindergarten to high school end, covers all relevant elements for the definition and establishment of educational institutions, the network of such institutions, their financing, legal identity and organisation, while also setting out the objectives of educational programmes. The law is thus the basis for all other specialised vertical laws. The amendments and additions of the ZOFVI adopted in 2007 introduced solutions to the law on the development of awareness of nationality, national identity and behaviour about the history of Slovenia and its culture. This amendment to the ZOFVI was also followed by an amendment to the Law on Primary School in 2007, which renamed compulsory subjects of Ethics and civic education to Ethics, homeland and civic education. In 2013, this law was re-amended when the subject was renamed Ethika, native and civic education. Despite the record in the legislation, practice shows that the development of awareness of nationality, patriotism, national identity and behaviour about the history of Slovenia and its culture is inconsistent with the marking of the national holidays, which are part of the implementation of the Hemisphere Republic of Slovenia, and with other activities that take place during that period, which is too short for the in-depth development of the awareness of belonging and love of the country. It is estimated that a more in-depth approach to the subject should be made and that these processes should be integrated into the life of the school in all areas. In particular, in primary school, cities are seen, and in secondary schools, in recent times, very worrying appear when students are presented with totalitarian symbols as something positive; thus, the phenomenon of totalitarianism is something acceptable, even positive. Such practice must not only be interrupted, but should be implemented in schools on the basis of European and civil values. Therefore, fundamental values such as patriotism and awareness of fundamental human rights must be included not only in occasional events, school events, but also in the curriculum as such. This means that these values should also be included in the curricula of all subjects in kindergarten, primary school and secondary school. The proposal for a law on amendments and additions to the ZOFVI amends the provisions on the objectives of education and education and adds a new indent to Article 2 of this Act, which provides that awareness should also be raised against all types of totalitarianism. The law in Article 12 also redefines the obligation to determine ways of integrating patriotism into educational processes. Furthermore, Article 86 of the ZOFVI provides that the private school implementing the programmes referred to in paragraph 1 of this Article shall be provided with 85% of the funds provided by the State or the local community for the implementation of the public school programmes. On 4 December 2014, the Constitutional Court ruled that the first sentence of Article 86, paragraph 2, of the Law on the organisation and financing of education and education in the workplace relating to public education programmes in private schools is inconsistent with the Constitution. We therefore propose that the provision in Article 86 should be amended and that private schools should be aligned with funding in public schools under a 100 % quota. The Council of Europe stated in its 2009 paper on Resolution 9004, that the Council of Europe and the Member States must provide an educational system that ensures equal opportunities and high quality education for all students to provide such knowledge and values as to develop and promote fundamental rights and democratic citizenship. They must also ensure the fundamental right to freedom of choice in education, in accordance with Article 2 of the Additional Protocol to the European Convention on Human Rights. Thus, the amendments to the ZOFVI Act follow the Constitutional Court's decision while at the same time allowing for the integration of patriotic content into primary school, secondary school, all subjects, all content, activities of interest and the whole school space.
ParlaMint-SI_2015-03-31-SDZ7-Redna-06.ana.u83	Thank you very much. I give the floor to the representative of the Government, Minister of Education, Science and Sport mag. Claudius Marquez.
ParlaMint-SI_2015-03-31-SDZ7-Redna-06.ana.u84	Thank you so much for saying that. Well, hello, all of you! I would like to give the opinion of the Government of the Republic of Slovenia on the Proposal for a Law amending and supplementing the Law on the organisation and financing of education and education submitted to the National Assembly by the Group of Members and Members. I would like to say that on 24 February 2015, a group of Members, with first-signed Jozet Tank, tabled a proposal for a law on amendments and additions to the Law on the organisation and financing of education and education. The Government of the Republic of Slovenia opposes that proposal, which is justified by the following. First of all, I would like to point out that the proposed amendment to Article 86(2) of the Law on the organisation and financing of education and education, hereinafter referred to as the ZOFVI Turmin, refer to the Constitutional Court Decision, U-l-269/12-24 of 4 December 2014, which concluded that the first sentence of Article 86 of the ZOFVI is in the work relating to publicly established primary education programmes, in breach of the Constitution. With the proposed amendment, which provides that private primary schools implementing the programme referred to in Article 86(1) of the ZOFVI shall be provided with 100 per cent of the funding allocated by the State or the local community for the implementation of the primary school programme; and discriminatory financing of private primary schools should be eliminated. In addition, the SDS Group proposed to supplement Article 2 of the ZOFVI, which sets out the objective of education and education with a new objective of education for free society by condemning all totalitarianism. In this context, it is also proposed to supplement Article 12 of the ZOFVI, which redefines the obligation to determine the way in which patriotism is involved in educational programmes. The new bill assesses that the financial implications of the proposed law at annual level would bring an additional EUR 390 000 for the state budget; of course, not during the period for which the state budget has already been adopted. These financial implications relate only to the amendment of Article 86 of the Education and Education Financing Act. Secondly, the Government of the Republic of Slovenia has examined the proposed amendment of the law and, with regard to the proposed amendments and additions, has adopted a position that the proposed solutions are not based on previous analyses, which are the basis for assessing the situation and preparing the main solutions to the changes and supplementing the law. The proposed changes relating to the financing of private primary schools are, in the view of the Government, flawed as private education problems do not fully address. They only summarise the decisions of the Constitutional Court in the work relating to the level of financing of private schools. In this way, they take into account only the aspect of private school funding and do not address the status of private schools and do not address issues relating to the adoption of private school programmes and the fulfilment of the conditions for their entry into the register of public education providers in the field of education and education. An amendment to the Law on Financing for Education and Education is already being prepared at the competent ministry, which will truly regulate the private education sector comprehensively in terms of financing private schools and the regulation of the status of private schools. With regard to the proposed supplement relating to the objectives of education and education, we stress that, despite the importance of developing awareness of nationality, national identity and knowledge of the history of Slovenia and its culture, a thorough analysis of the current situation in the field of existing education programmes in primary and secondary schools should be made prior to the adoption of that supplement. In this regard, it is also necessary to determine to what extent the proposed content is already covered by primary and secondary education programmes. Since the proposed solutions in the new ZOFVI only take into account the financing aspect of existing private schools, they do not represent the real financial consequences that such funding will have in the future. However, the new ZOFVI, which is being prepared at the Ministry of Education, will contain precisely those systemic solutions that will take account of the current fiscal situation. In view of the above, the Government of the Republic of Slovenia does not support the amendments and additions to the Law on the organisation and financing of education and education introduced by the Group of Members in the legislative process. The Government of the Republic of Slovenia will put in the legislative procedure a proposal for a law that will adequately regulate the topic submitted. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2015-03-31-SDZ7-Redna-06.ana.u88	Thank you very much. Your Honor! The SD, Desus Group, the United Left and the Alliance tabled a proposal to amend the Constitution in February this year. The purpose of the initiative is a clear distinction between public and private education. The proposed amendment to Article 86, which would provide 100 % of the funding of the public school compulsory school curriculum for private schools, is by no means the only change that would be expected in this debate, namely in the debate on the delimitation of public and private education. The question of whether we finance private institutions from public funds has already, as has been said, been twice employed by the Constitutional Court. In 2001, the court took a decision and I quote that. "The idea that parents are children who send children to private schools and have to pay tuition is not justified in relation to those whose children attend public schools from the point of view of the Constitution. The legislator's decision to finance the state in its entirety only public schools in which all parents can enter their children is in the field of its assessment and therefore is not contrary to the Constitution." In the Alliance, we will not be dealing with the question of whether it is, as some claim, a desire to privatise public education throughout the school vertical. Only the National Assembly, both the Alliance, has the right as founder to maintain in the Constitution the fundamental values of this company, which it has already enshrined in the Constitution in 1991; namely that the primary concern of the State in the field of primary education is the provision of public school funding from public funds. By supplementing the Constitution, we will only achieve that the Constitution states that public education is a value, as has already been interpreted in 2001, and will thus become the only form of primary education that has this kind of legal protection. Therefore, the proposal for constitutional amendments is without prejudice to the eluded relationship between public and private primary education, provides the same funding as before and will clearly oblige the legislator to take primary care of public education. We believe that the draft amendments to the Constitution support the principle of state neutrality and pluralism in the national educational system. In any event, it is without prejudice to the right to establish private schools and the possibility for these schools to form part of the national educational system. It is true, however, that it primarily ensures the survival and quality of the public school network. In the Alliance, therefore, we do not oppose the limited funding of private primary schools implementing the publicly applicable educational programme. Private schools, in our opinion, enrich the school space with different pedagogy or allow parents to train their children in institutions with the same global view that they are raising their children. However, we do not agree that the latter should impoverish the network of public primary schools. Private schools can therefore, in our view, be co-financed to a limited extent in the manner prescribed by the law. And if I touch on a little bit more civic education, when the awareness of belonging and national identity is brought up to children in kindergartens and later throughout education. It will probably be enough to say that different ministers, especially the right-hand side, had different ideas that they tried to enforce; however, it turns out that the results were not as expected. But we understand that the right-hand side is clearly tightening the muscles in showing patriotism in implementing ideas and proposing unusual proposals that are obviously not part of a law that should primarily address the financing of public and private education. In the Alliance, we believe that such content is already included in the curriculum and that the international comparative research carried out has shown that knowledge of the content mentioned is not so bad. These symbols are also linked to the roots of Slovenian statehood, and most of them see the main force in which the partisan struggle ensured the survival of the national community. There are probably a number of questions, as Mrs Bachič mentioned earlier, which is the difference between national and civil education. Domestic or patriotic education means developing awareness of national ownership and national identity and knowledge of the history of Slovenia, its tradition, culture. Civil education, however, includes the development of civil literacy, namely knowledge of the political system, human and civil rights and duties, the adoption of critical thinking and analytical skills and the promotion of participation and participation at the level of school, local communities and of course of the country. Studies carried out in this area, on the one hand, show a relatively high level of civil and domestic awareness; on the other hand, they highlight insufficient knowledge in the area of civil literacy, where knowledge is particularly based on some of the grounds of civil literacy at a rather low level. However, reducing civic education only to domestic education is not a prudent decision because they are inseparably linked. The proposed amendments to this Act do not mention any strengthening of these content of civic education. Since the area can be a very good springboard for ideological debates, the objectives of home and civic education must be approached with full objectivity and, in particular, criticism. According to young people, the qualities that best define an adult patriotic citizen, his knowledge and protection of his rights, his respectful attitude towards the Slovenian language, his willingness to respect the Constitution and the laws and his non-discriminatory attitude towards fellow citizens. In the secondary school system, home education in addition to the content of the various social subjects is provided for in the compulsory selection content of a civil culture which takes place only 15 hours but which is the closest to home education. In the debate on the Committee, I have already mentioned that I have been teaching at the high school a subject which covered these objectives and the content of patriotic education. Public schools are accessible to all and are established by both the State and the local community with a view to providing quality and accessible public education, and this has been achieved through a careful school policy at the heart of which to allow each individual, regardless of his or her initial position, to gain all the potential for further life in an ideologically unburdened environment. To this end, a public network of schools has been developed, which, of course, is complemented by private institutions. So all parents are allowed to enroll their children in free primary education programmes in the public network. At the same time, it is not possible to overlook the provision of the Constitution in Article 57, which provides that education is free. Freedom of education at constitutional level guarantees individuals and legal persons that they also establish private schools. Thus, the State creates the necessary legal frameworks for the establishment and functioning of private schools and recognises the public education acquired in private schools. However, it follows from the decision of the Constitutional Court in 2001 that the contribution to finance depends on how much this State can do, but must be supported in such a way as to enable the actual existence of a private school. Any thought that parents who enter their children in private schools would be in an unequal situation does not exist in this place. I note, therefore, that the current system of financing education and education is fully adequate, and that 85% of the public school programme is financed by private schools. Every parent who enrolls his or her child in a private school wants a different system of education provided by public schools for his or her child, so it is also right that they spend some of their financial resources for this part. ParlaMint-SI_2015-03-31-SDZ7-Redna-06.ana.u165	Dear President, honourable Members and Members of Parliament, welcome! On 16 January this year, the Ministry of Justice informed the President of the Republic of Slovenia that five candidates and one candidate, Dr. Boštjan M. Zupančič, Dr. Marko Ilešič, a special professor, Dr. Slobodan Dujić, Professor Dr. Verica Trstenjak, a special professor, Dr. Marko Bošnjak and Lucian Bmbič, had applied for a post of candidate or candidate for a judge or judge at the Court of Justice in Luxembourg. The President of the Republic obtained the opinion of the Government of the Republic of Slovenia and the opinion of the Council of Justice, in accordance with the Law on the Proposal of Candidates from the Republic of Slovenia for Judges of International Courts. Both opinions state that all candidates meet formal conditions, while the Council of Justice gave priority to the candidate in the following order: Dr. Marko Ilešič, Dr. Boštjan M. Zupančič, Prof. Verica Trstenjak. After examining all the proposals received and following consultations with the leaders of the parliamentary groups in the National Assembly, the President of the Republic decided to propose two candidates, Dr Mark Ilešič and Prof. Verico Trstenjak, under the sixth section of the Act, to be one candidate or candidate for a judge or judge at the Court of Justice of the European Union in Luxembourg. The President of the Republic estimates that both candidates, both the proposed candidate and the proposed candidate, with their competence, experience and reputation at home and in the world meet the high professional criteria for carrying out this important function. In view of the announcement by the President of the National Assembly that the candidate Prof. Verica Trstenjak has informed the President of the withdrawal from the candidacy, allow me to explain further the proposal by the President of the Republic for candidate Dr Mark Ilešić. Dr Marko Ilešič has been a judge of the Court of Justice of the European Union since 2004. From 2012 to 2014, the President of the Third Chamber of the Court, one year earlier, was the President of the Fifth Chamber. He was a member of the Senate and a Judge-Rapporteur in a number of important cases that ended with rebuttal judgments. As a rapporteur, he is particularly active in the areas of institutional law, EU law, economic law, personal data protection, consumer protection, transport law and private international law. Prior to his judicial office, he served as an EU court candidate since 1970 at the Faculty of Law in Ljubljana, and between 2001 and 2004, he was a dean, and between 1995 and 2001. He was an arbitrator of several arbitrations. He is active in many domestic, international professional organisations. In 2014 he also received the highest recognition of the Association of Associations of Lawyers of Slovenia, a recognition for life's work. Since 1985 he has been actively involved in sports activities. Among other things, he was President and Vice-President of the Football Federation of Slovenia, a member of the Court of Appeal of the World Football Association and the European Football Association. He has been engaged in civil, economic and international private law for more than 40 years. Its bibliography covers 428 bibliographic units by 2014. The candidate, with his extremely rich scientific opus and work both at home and abroad, strengthened his professional reputation and significantly helped to raise the reputation of the Republic of Slovenia in the wider legal profession as well as the lay public. He is active in English, French, Italian, German, Polish, Croatian and Serbian, and passively in Latin, Portuguese, Spanish and other Slovenian languages. On the basis of all the above, the President of the Republic considers that the candidate can undoubtedly justify very high professional expectations in the performance of this proposed important function. Thank you very much.
